certify that Claude isn't used in their workflows.</li></ul><p><strong>Friction point: </strong>Cutting ties would require the Pentagon to have a replacement ready for Claude, which is currently the only model used in classified systems.</p><ul><li>The use of Claude in the Venezuela operation came through Anthropic's partnership with Palantir, the AI software company.</li><li>It's also used for a wide variety of more bureaucratic functions within the military.</li></ul><p><strong>What to watch: </strong>Elon Musk's <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/02/23/ai-defense-department-deal-musk-xai-grok">xAI recently signed</a> a contract to bring its model, Grok, into classified settings, though it's unclear whether it would be able to fully replace Claude.</p><ul><li>The Pentagon has been speeding up conversations with OpenAI and Google about moving their models — already available for unclassified uses — into classified systems, sources tell Axios.</li><li>One source familiar with the discussions said that right now, it appears Claude is ahead of the others in a number of applications relevant to the military, such as offensive cyber capabilities.</li><li>The one source said Gemini is seen as a potential replacement if and when a deal is reached. That would require Google to let the Pentagon uses its model for "all lawful purposes," the same terms that Anthropic rejected.</li></ul><p><strong>Go deeper:</strong> <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/02/19/anthropic-pentagon-ai-fight-openai-google-xai">Pentagon-Anthropic battle pushes other AI labs into major dilemma</a></p></blockquote><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JdKyWmzfv8EeBuxHC/exclusive-hegseth-gives-anthropic-until-friday-to-back-down-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Large-Scale Online Deanonymization with LLMs](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xwCWyy8RvAKsSoBRF/large-scale-online-deanonymization-with-llms)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 24, 2026 5:02 PM GMT<br /><br /><p><b><strong>TL;DR:</strong></b><span> We show that LLM agents can figure out who you are from your anonymous online posts. Across Hacker News, Reddit, LinkedIn, and anonymized interview transcripts, our method identifies users with high precision – and scales to tens of thousands of candidates.</span></p><p><span>While it has been known that individuals can be uniquely identified by surprisingly few attributes, this was often practically limited. Data is often only available in unstructured form and deanonymization used to require human investigators to search and reason based on clues. We show that from a handful of comments, LLMs can infer where you live, what you do, and your interests – then search for you on the web. In our new research, we show that this is not only possible but increasingly practical. </span></p><p><b><strong>Paper:</strong></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800" rel="noreferrer"><span> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800" rel="noreferrer"><span>Large-Scale Online Deanonymization with LLMs</span></a></p><img alt="agentic_deanon_example.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1771867661/lexical_client_uploads/wyiogfjevxkcgpgt1wrt.png" /><h2><span>Motivation – Why do we research this?</span></h2><p><span>Among the near-term effects of AI, different forms of </span><a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2026/02/18/anthropic-pentagon-ai-defense-war-surveillance.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>AI surveillance</span></a><span> pose some of the most concrete harms. It is already known that </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07298" rel="noreferrer"><span>LLMs can infer personal attributes</span></a><span> about authors and use that to create biographical profiles of individuals (also </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01645" rel="noreferrer"><span>see</span></a><span>). Such profiles can be misused straightforwardly with </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0957417426004598" rel="noreferrer"><span>spear-phishing</span></a><span> or many other forms of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11449" rel="noreferrer"><span>monetizing exploits</span></a><span>. Using AI for massively scalable "</span><a href="https://exa.ai/docs/changelog/people-search-launch" rel="noreferrer"><span>people search</span></a><span>" is harmful by itself by undermining many privacy assumptions.</span></p><p><span>Beyond shining a light on this growing harmful use of AI, we explore options on how individuals can protect themselves – and what social platforms and AI labs can do in response.</span></p><p><span>We acknowledge that by publishing our results and approximate methods, we carry some risk of accelerating misuse developments. Nevertheless, we believe that publishing is the right decision.</span></p><h2><span>How We Designed Our </span><span>Benchmarks</span></h2><p><span>It is tricky to benchmark LLMs on deanonymization. You don't want to actually deanonymize anonymous individuals. And there is no ground truth for online deanonymization – how could you verify that the AI found the correct person?</span></p><p><span>Our solution is to construct two types of deanonymization proxies which allow us to study the effectiveness of LLMs at these tasks. We also perform a real world deanonymization attack on the Anthropic Interviewer dataset with manual verification.</span></p><h3><span>Proxy 1: Cross-Platform Matching</span></h3><p><span>The idea of our cross-platform benchmark is to take two accounts on different platforms for which we know they belong together, and then remove any directly identifiable features from one of the accounts. The task is then to put the accounts of the same person back together.</span></p><p><span>Concretely, we take non-anonymous Hacker News (HN) accounts that link to their LinkedIn. We then anonymize the HN accounts, removing all directly identifying information. Then, we le</span><span>t LLMs match the anonymized</span><span> account to the true person. We find that we can re-identify most accounts with high precision when we are combining search and reasoning. We use embeddings-based search to find the 100 most promising candidates and then reason to select and verify the most promising candidate. (See </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Section 4</span></a><span> of the paper for full details on the HN-LinkedIn experiment.) </span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 63.94779771615008%;"><img alt="hn_pr_curve.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1771867257/lexical_client_uploads/husizoo9aytv9gsnbign.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Search refers to using LLMs to extract information and using search over embeddings to find the correct match. Search+Reason adds an LLM step that selects and verifies the correct match.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span>Proxy 2: Matching Split Accounts</span></h3><p><span>As another approach to benchmarking deanonymization, we artificially split up a single account into two accounts and then test if we are able to link these split accounts back together. On Reddit, we split user histories into "before" and "after," then test whether LLMs can link them back together. Alternatively, we split Reddit accounts by community, dividing their activity according to the subreddits they participate in. In both cases, LLM embeddings combined with reasoning significantly outperform </span><a href="https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1109/SP.2008.33"><span>Netflix-Prize-style</span></a><span> baselines that match based on subreddit activity patterns and metadata. (See </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Section 5</span></a><span> for the community-split experiment and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Section 6</span></a><span> for the temporal-split experiment.)</span></p><h3><span>It Scales to Much Larger Datasets</span></h3><p><span>As candidate pools grow to tens of thousands, LLM-based attacks degrade gracefully at high precision. This implies that with sufficient compute, these methods would already scale to entire platforms. With future models, we can expect that performance will improve while the cost will only go down. (See </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Section 4.3</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Section 6.4</span></a><span> for our scaling analyses.)</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.82626427406199%;"><img alt="hn_scaling.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1771866977/lexical_client_uploads/gka27cosvyxfa4m9qmhj.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>We scale the candidate pool size and extrapolate to 100M users. Recall@90% Precision</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Identifying people in the real world</span></h2><p><span>Anthropic's </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/Anthropic/AnthropicInterviewer"><span>Interviewer dataset</span></a><span> contains anonymized interviews with scientists about their use of AI. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05918"><span>Li (2026)</span></a><span> first showed that a simple LLM agent could re-identify some of these scientists just by searching the web and reasoning over the transcripts. Our agent is able to identify 9 out of 125 individuals in the dataset</span><span>, though we caveat that this </span><span>number is based on manual verification, since no ground truth data exists for this task. (See </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Section 2</span></a><span> for our agentic deanonymization experiments.)</span></p><img alt="anthropic_example.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1771866941/lexical_client_uploads/bckrzd4eknfqzeyuobru.png" /><h2><span>What Now?</span></h2><p><b><strong>What can platforms do?</strong></b><span> The most effective short-term mitigation is restricting data access. Enforcing rate limits on API access to user data, detecting automated scraping, and restricting bulk data exports all raise the cost of large-scale attacks. Platforms should assume that pseudonymous users can be linked across accounts and to real identities, and this should inform their data access policies.</span></p><p><b><strong>What can LLM providers do?</strong></b><span> Refusal guardrails and usage monitoring can help, but both have significant limitations. Our deanonymization framework splits an attack into seemingly benign tasks – summarizing profiles, computing embeddings, ranking candidates – that individually look like normal usage, making misuse hard to detect. Refusals can be bypassed through task decomposition. And none of these mitigations apply to open-source models, where safety guardrails can be removed and there is no usage monitoring at all. In some tested scenarios, LLM agents did refuse to help us but this could be avoided with small prompt changes. This mirrors inherent issues with preventing AI misuse – each step of misuse can locally be identical or very similar to valid use cases.</span></p><p><b><strong>What should you do if you are using pseudonymous accounts online?</strong></b><span> Individuals may adopt a stronger security mindset regarding privacy. Each piece of specific information you share – your city, your job, a conference you attended, a niche hobby – narrows down who you could be. The combination is often a unique fingerprint. Ask yourself: could a team of smart investigators figure out who you are from your posts? If yes, LLM agents can likely do the same, and the cost of doing so is only going down.</span></p><p><b><strong>Paper:</strong></b><span> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800"><span>Large-Scale Online Deanonymization with LLMs</span></a></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xwCWyy8RvAKsSoBRF/large-scale-online-deanonymization-with-llms#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Open sourcing a browser extension that shows when people are wrong on the internet](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/open-sourcing-a-browser-extension-that-shows-when-people-are)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 24, 2026 4:36 PM GMT<br /><br /><figure class="image"><img alt="Screenshot From 2026-02-22 14-58-40 bordered-shadow.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1771801565/lexical_client_uploads/gqxuyfkozrs4psnuiv01.png" /><figcaption><i>Example of OpenErrata nitting the Sequences</i></figcaption></figure><p>I just published <a href="https://github.com/ZeroPathAI/OpenErrata">OpenErrata</a>, a browser extension that investigates the posts you read using your OpenAI API key, and underlines any factual claims that are sourceably incorrect. It then saves the results of the investigation so that whenever anybody else using the extension visits the post (with or without an API key), they get the corrections on their first visit.</p><p>I've noticed that while people can theoretically paste everything they're reading into ChatGPT for verification:</p><ul><li>No one has the time to do that</li><li>It duplicates work between readers</li><li>It takes around 5 minutes to get a really good sourced response for most mid-length posts.&nbsp;</li></ul><p>I figure most of LessWrong is reading the same stuff, and that if a good portion of the community begins using this or something like it, we can avoid these problems.</p><p>Here is OpenErrata at work on some LessWrong &amp; Substack articles that were published within the last week. I was a little surprised at what a high percentage of the articles I read seem to have at least one or two errors, even with how conservative <a href="https://github.com/ZeroPathAI/OpenErrata/blob/main/src/typescript/api/src/lib/investigators/prompt.ts">my prompt</a> is. When I delete rows from the database and rerun, often it finds different (and valid) ones it didn't find the first time:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="OpenErrata highlighting an incorrect claim on Astral Codex Ten with a hover tooltip showing the correction and source" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/xogyunaajgkbc0u4zw6c" /><figcaption><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/record-low-crime-rates-are-real-not">"Record Low Crime Rates Are Real, Not Just Reporting Bias Or Improved Medical Care"&nbsp;</a></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/r8p7qlqj0sh9doetzejr" /><figcaption><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FreZTE9Bc7reNnap7/life-at-the-frontlines-of-demographic-collapse">Life at the Frontlines of Demographic Collapse</a></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/p0ikffhmrhrm16uw38nl" /><figcaption><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qefrWyeiMvWEFRitN/be-skeptical-of-milestone-announcements-by-young-ai-startups">Be skeptical of milestone announcements by young AI startups</a></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/mousflqpi3pweydmywkh" /><figcaption><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioZxrP7BhS5ArK59w/did-claude-3-opus-align-itself-via-gradient-hacking">Did Claude 3 Opus align itself via gradient hacking?</a> (Note: as pointed out by commenters, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/open-sourcing-a-browser-extension-that-shows-when-people-are?commentId=eYTiut5uHnztxLFP6">this correction is incorrect</a>. Leaving it up as it still seems interesting as a statement about the models.)</figcaption></figure><p>The project is published under my company, but the entire thing is self-hostable and AGPLv3 licensed. I also made an API available so that providers can use the results for articles independently and do statistics on them/embed them. Some future additions I &amp; others could work on:</p><ul><li>A website for 'leaderboards'/'loserboards', viewing in progress investigations, helpful-to-the-reader reputation mechanics, etc.</li><li>Reasoning for no-nit results.</li><li>An appeal process that is completely AI driven, so that you can talk to the AI to point out either additional ways articles are wrong, or reasons previous nits are incorrect, which are reflected in the results. I think it should be possible to figure out how to make that adversarially robust as the tool gets better.</li><li>Support for other sites (NYT, Wikipedia, Reddit, Nitter, etc.) Right now it only works on LessWrong/Substack and X (sort of).<ul><li>Better support for X/Twitter; I've got some ideas for ways the investigator could actually access related tweets and sources, for example.</li></ul></li><li>Support for comments.</li></ul><p>I really enjoyed working on &amp; using this and want to keep doing so, so let me know if you like it/find it useful!</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iMw7qhtZGNFxMRD4H/open-sourcing-a-browser-extension-that-shows-when-people-are#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The ML ontology and the alignment ontology](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Yz4YHncz2vwN4ksDA/the-ml-ontology-and-the-alignment-ontology)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 24, 2026 4:39 AM GMT<br /><br /><p><span>This post contains some rough reflections on the alignment community trying to make its ontology legible to the mainstream ML community, and the lessons we should take from that experience.</span></p><p><span>Historically, it was difficult for the alignment community to engage with the ML community because the alignment community was using a fundamentally different ontology—featuring concepts like inner vs outer alignment, mesa-optimizers, corrigibility, situational awareness, and so on. Even a concept as simple as "giving an AI an instruction in natural language" often threw a kind of type error in ML researchers' ontologies, in which goals were meant to be specified by setting agents' reward functions.</span></p><p><span>The concept of situational awareness is another one which doesn't really make sense in the classic ML ontology. My impression is that Ilya starting to take situational awareness seriously (after Ajeya gave a talk about it at OpenAI) was one of the main drivers of his transition to alignment research. Unfortunately, Ilya's subsequent research on </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/weak-to-strong-generalization/" rel="noreferrer"><span>weak-to-strong generalization</span></a><span> stayed pretty stuck in the ML ontology, which in my opinion made it unpromising from the get-go. (I don't remember if I stated this publicly at the time, but I was pretty critical internally inside OpenAI, especially to Collin Burns. In hindsight I wish I'd clearly stated publicly that I wasn't very excited about the research.)</span></p><p><span>These are two of many examples over the last few years of the alignment ontology winning out over the ML ontology by being better at describing LLMs. In response, the ML ontology has expanded to include concepts like "giving AIs instructions" and "situational awareness", but not in any principled way—it's sort of shoehorned them in without most people noticing the confusion. (E.g. if you ask </span><i><em>why</em></i><span> the AIs are following instructions, or how situational awareness might develop, I think most ML researchers would give you pretty confused answers.)</span></p><p><span>Historically, it was sometimes possible to make alignment concepts legible in the ML ontology before compelling empirical evidence arose, but it was a typically a very laborious and unrewarding process. ML researchers would raise objections that felt extremely nitpicky from the alignment ontology. In part this was due to the difficulty of communicating across ontologies, but in part it was also due to motivated reasoning to find reasons to reject claims made by alignment proponents (e.g. I think </span><a href="https://medium.com/@francois.chollet/the-impossibility-of-intelligence-explosion-5be4a9eda6ec" rel="noreferrer"><span>this post from Chollet</span></a><span> is a pretty good example). Even when ML researchers agreed that an alignment concept made sense in principle, it was usually hard for them to then propagate the consequences into the rest of their ontology—in part because doing so would have had big implications for their identity and career plans.</span></p><p><span>Meanwhile, the alignment community would waste time, and sometimes make itself more confused, by trying to adapt their concepts to make more sense to ML researchers. "</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.01790" rel="noreferrer"><span>Goal misgeneralization</span></a><span>" is a good example of this, since the problem of inner misalignment is more that correct generalization isn't a well-defined concept, than that the agent will learn to "misgeneralize". MIRI's paper on </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/FormalizingConvergentGoals.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>Formalizing Convergent Instrumental Goals</span></a><span> seems like it also wasn't very useful, especially compared with their other research (though unlike goal misgeneralization I doubt it made many people more confused). Owain Evans' "</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.00667" rel="noreferrer"><span>out-of-context reasoning</span></a><span>" is a case that I'm less confident about, since it does seem like putting the idea in ML terms has helped him and others do interesting empirical research on it.</span></p><p><span>I did a lot of this myself too, to be clear. "Trying to make alignment concepts legible in the ML ontology" was in some sense the main goal of my </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.00626" rel="noreferrer"><span>alignment problem from a deep learning perspective</span></a><span> paper, and I've updated significantly downwards on its value since starting to think in these terms. In hindsight, the main thing I would've told my past self (and the rest of the alignment community) is to pay less attention to the ML ontology. Unfortunately, my sense is that OpenPhil and various other groups (including my past self) pushed pretty hard for engagement with the ML ontology, which I count as a significant mistake.</span></p><p><span>There are still ways that engaging with the ML community would have been valuable—I think mainstream ML researchers are good at pushing alignment researchers to be more precise and more grounded in the existing literature. But broadly speaking it would've been better to have treated alignment ideas like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/R6M4vmShiowDn56of/butterfly-ideas" rel="noreferrer"><span>butterfly ideas</span></a><span> which would be harmed by premature exposure to ML thinking</span><i><em>.</em></i></p><hr /><p><span>I suspect that many AI safety researchers will resonate with the broad outlines of what I've discussed above. Below is the part that I expect will be more controversial.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately much of the alignment community today seems to be in an analogous position to the ML community during the 2010s. Concepts like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FuGfR3jL3sw6r8kB4/richard-ngo-s-shortform?commentId=ixQRCdJZz6xTB8uyb" rel="noreferrer"><span>scheming</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PWHkMac9Xve6LoMJy/alignment-faking-frame-is-somewhat-fake-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>alignment faking</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/67fNBeHrjdrZZNDDK/defining-alignment-research" rel="noreferrer"><span>alignment research</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FuGfR3jL3sw6r8kB4/richard-ngo-s-shortform?commentId=sAhSxKLu39Y6ky4SG" rel="noreferrer"><span>strategy research</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kcKrE9mzEHrdqtDpE/the-case-for-ensuring-that-powerful-ais-are-controlled?commentId=2HZCnmo32ZX2Dmsxo" rel="noreferrer"><span>P(doom)</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4v3uqWeVmco" rel="noreferrer"><span>misuse vs misalignment</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://x.com/richardmcngo/status/2026138292383887730?s=46" rel="noreferrer"><span>AGI timelines</span></a><span>, and so on seem to me to be sufficiently vague and/or confused that it's hard to think clearly about AGI when they're important parts of your ontology.</span></p><p><span>This is a pretty broad claim, so let me be a little more specific. Suppose we very roughly divide the AI safety community into the parts that are more EA-affiliated (most lab safety teams, most orgs working out of Constellation, OpenPhil, etc) and the parts that are more Less Wrong affiliated (e.g. almost everyone on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wn5jTrtKkhspshA4c/michaeldickens-s-shortform?commentId=zoBMvdMAwpjTEY4st" rel="noreferrer"><span>Habryka's list of individuals in this comment</span></a><span>). I think my diagnosis above is partially true of LW safety, but strongly true of EA safety. The people who are generating novel and important AGI-related concepts are almost all pretty decoupled from EA safety, even though that's where most of the money and jobs are:</span></p><ul><li><span>There's a line of thinking which understands models in terms of their personas and identities. I'd point to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/N7nDePaNabJdnbXeE/p/vJFdjigzmcXMhNTsx" rel="noreferrer"><span>Janus</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioZxrP7BhS5ArK59w/did-claude-3-opus-align-itself-via-gradient-hacking" rel="noreferrer"><span>Fiora</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3EzbtNLdcnZe8og8b/the-void-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>nostalgebraist</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/D7PumeYTDPfBTp3i7/the-waluigi-effect-mega-post" rel="noreferrer"><span>Cleo Nardo</span></a><span> as some EA safety "outsiders" who have developed ideas in this space. While some of these concepts are getting picked up within EA safety (e.g. by </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/persona-selection-model" rel="noreferrer"><span>Anthropic's interpretability team</span></a><span>), the ontology gap is still large enough to cause </span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2014155341274210390?s=46" rel="noreferrer"><span>adversarial dynamics</span></a><span>.</span></li><li><a href="https://x.com/richardmcngo/status/2025001871782674552?s=46" rel="noreferrer"><span>MIRI's former agent foundations team</span></a><span> did (and continues to do) a lot of great thinking, despite EA safety's strong skepticism of agent foundations.</span></li><li><span>Jan Kulveit and his collaborators are probably the people most closely affiliated with EA safety who I think are doing strong novel thinking (although </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FuGfR3jL3sw6r8kB4/richard-ngo-s-shortform?commentId=ejoL2TvK7LKYpaD3K" rel="noreferrer"><span>I suspect that their thinking would be better if they decoupled more</span></a><span>).</span></li><li><a href="https://naturalhazard.xyz/ben_jess_sarah_starter_pack" rel="noreferrer"><span>Michael Vassar and his collaborators</span></a><span> are doing an excellent job at understanding the sociopolitical dynamics governing both society at large and also the AI safety community. This cluster is decoupled both from EA safety and from LW safety.</span></li></ul><p><span>Related to the last point: I personally feel pretty decoupled from LW safety in part because LW safety is focusing a lot on AI governance these days, but has a very different ontology for thinking about politics </span><a href="https://21civ.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>than I do</span></a><span>. In fact, I originally started writing this post as an analogy for how I relate to the LessWrong community with regards to politics. However, ontology gaps in alignment seem sufficiently important that I decided to make this post solely about them, and save the analogizing to politics for a separate post or shortform.</span></p><p><span>To sum up my main takeaways: while working in the dominant ontology may seem like the "safest" and most reputable bet, real progress comes from building out a different ontology to the point where it can replace the old one. Good luck to anyone who's trying to do that!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Yz4YHncz2vwN4ksDA/the-ml-ontology-and-the-alignment-ontology#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Bioanchors 2: Electric Bacilli](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wgqcExv9AgN5MuJuY/bioanchors-2-electric-bacilli)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 24, 2026 1:07 AM GMT<br /><br /><p><em><a href="https://tsvibt.blogspot.com/2026/02/bioanchors-2-electric-bacilli_23.html">Crosspost from my blog</a>.</em></p>
<p><em>[Previously: "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sTDfraZab47KiRMmT/views-on-when-agi-comes-and-on-strategy-to-reduce">Views on when AGI comes and on strategy to reduce existential risk</a>", "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5tqFT3bcTekvico4d/do-confident-short-timelines-make-sense">Do confident short timelines make sense?</a>"]</em></p>
<p><em>[Whenever discussing when AGI will come, it bears repeating: If anyone builds AGI, everyone dies; no one knows when AGI will be made, whether soon or late; a bunch of people and orgs are trying to make it; and they should stop and be stopped.]</em></p>
<h1>Arguments for fast AGI progress</h1>
<p>Many arguments about "when will AGI come" focus on reasons to think progress will continue quickly, such as:</p>
<ul>
<li>Line go up.</li>
<li>Researchers can pivot to address new obstacles and ditch dead ends.</li>
<li>AI can be used to accelerate AI research.</li>
<li>We're over a threshold of economic returns, such that AI research will permanently see much more investment than before.</li>
</ul>
<h1>Intuition pumps for being close to AGI</h1>
<p>These are all valid and truly worrying. But, to say anything specific and confident about when (in clock time) AGI will come, we'd also have to know how fast progress is being made in an absolute sense; specifically, in an absolute sense as measured by "how much of what you need to make AGI do you have".</p>
<p>There are various intuition pumps / analogies that people use to inform their sense of how far AI research has come. For example:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>AI is like a child developing.</strong> Progress is made incrementally and continually; each year brings a new quantity and quality of progress that builds on but transcends the previous. We match AI capabilities to the age of a human with the closest capabilities set. We find that AI grows by 2 years per year (or something), is currently approximately a grad student, and will soon be a leading researcher. Some months later it becomes mildly superhuman.</li>
<li><strong>AI is like brains and neurons.</strong> We need a bunch of compute power. We measure how much computing power we have against how much computing power a brain uses. We calculate tradeoffs between performance gains from algorithmic progress vs. from more compute. When we have enough effective compute, we get human level AI or beyond.</li>
<li><strong>AI is like an employee.</strong> At first it is not even worth the time to manage it; then it is helpful on some narrow tasks; then it can do a wide range of common / low-ish skill tasks, with questionable reliability; then it becomes reliable for easy things and starts contributing on difficult tasks and becomes economically valuable; then it starts replacing whole jobs; then it starts replacing whole companies / sectors; then it starts generating new sectors.</li>
<li><strong>AI is like a student.</strong> We feed it more training data and run more reps; it gets higher test scores; once it performs like the very best students, it becomes ready to do real research.</li>
</ul>
<p>I believe these are poor intuition pumps for understanding when AGI comes because they do not evoke the sense that there is some unknown, probably-large blob of complexity that one has to possess in order to make AGI. They paper over differences in <em>how</em> the AI system does what it does.</p>
<h1>Synthetic life as an intuition pump</h1>
<p>Intuition pumps can only go so far. Each domain has its own central complexities, and there's no good reason that the world has to present a deeply correct analogy for the development of AGI, and in fact I'm not aware of such. That said, as long as we're doing intuition pumps, I want to propose another intuition pump for timelines on progress on a very complex task: synthetic life. We use the analogy:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>human minds : AGI :: natural bacteria : synthetic bacteria</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Specifically, we can compare the general task of making AGI (which, to be clear, is a ~maximally bad thing to do) as analogous to:</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>the task of producing, artificially from scratch, a bacterium-like object that has all the impressive capabilities of biotic bacteria</strong>, such as growing and dividing, self-repairing, avoiding toxins and predators, evolving novel complex characteristics to perform well in many different niches, competing against other life for space and resources, etc., <strong>but that is "very unlike" natural bacteria / is produced "very independently from" natural bacteria</strong>.</p>
</blockquote>
<h1>Some things I like about this analogy</h1>
<ul>
<li><strong>There are big blobs of algorithmic complexity / understanding / ideas.</strong> Specifically, there's the genome. More abstractly, there's the ideas in the genome (e.g. chemical pathways, abstracted from specific enzymes).</li>
<li><strong>Evolution poured a huge amount of experimentation into getting that big blob of ideas.</strong></li>
<li><strong>That big blob of ideas is not fully accessible / usable for us, just because we can read it off in some form.</strong>
<ul>
<li>We can read a bacterial genome, or a brain connectome, but that doesn't mean we can design another bacterium / mind that uses those ideas (except in a pretty narrow cheaty way, at best). (I think this is less true for synlife, because we really do understand somewhat more about genomes compared to minds, and can extract more abstract ideas from genomes compared to from, say, connectomes. It's far from a perfect analogy.)</li>
<li>We know that evolution worked by selection on variation in the DNA sequence. But that doesn't mean we can get evolution's results:
<ul>
<li>Doing it evolution's way is crazy slow / compute intensive.</li>
<li>It's not easy to get ahold of evolution's training signal; the training signal is complex, subtle, and high compute. Poor replacements for that signal get incremental gains but don't get the deeper gains.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>The big blob of ideas contains a bunch of superfluous stuff</strong>: Redundant mechanisms, random damage / suboptimal settings, functional but kinda arbitrary choices (with lots of similarly good alternatives), mechanisms with unnecessary functions.
<ul>
<li>Therefore, we expect to have much less total work to do than evolution did in evolving bacteria. (And AI researchers have much less total work than evolution for making human minds.)</li>
<li>However, we expect to have some large amount of total work to do.</li>
<li>And, we cannot tell which is which.</li>
<li>And, the fact that we can think/design/experiment/compress more abstractly and efficiently than evolution, and can avoid a bunch of the work, does not say that much about how close we are, because the default is that there's a big blob that you have to invent.</li>
<li>Just because we <em>could</em> bypass a bunch of algorithmic complexity, doesn't mean we magically do so. You'd still have to figure out how to do so.</li>
<li>Retreating to "bitter-lesson" type arguments/plans also retreats away from arguments that we're doing things more efficiently than evolution.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>It's not exactly clear what counts as success, but it feels like there's some big accomplishment or bundle of big accomplishments that would qualify, and a bunch of cool-but-not-successful things one could do.</strong>
<ul>
<li>(I think this is more true in the case of synthetic life than AGI. For AGI, we basically mean "controls the lightcone". For synthetic life, we could mean various things like "is made from entirely synthetic elements (as opposed to just synthetic DNA inserted into a living cell)" or "doesn't use any normal proteins" or "doesn't use DNA" or similar. It's far from a perfect analogy.)</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>There are counterintuitive progress curves.</strong>
<ul>
<li>There are things that sound like huge progress / most of the way there, but they don't necessarily imply that you're anywhere near some later milestone.</li>
<li>For example, in 2010 the J. Craig Venter Institute produced a "synthetic cell" (that is, a cell whose genome was synthesized by stitching together chemically assembled DNA segments). In 2016, they did the same thing but they also deleted a bunch of DNA, "<a href="https://www.jcvi.org/research/first-minimal-synthetic-bacterial-cell">making it the smallest genome of any self-replicating organism</a>". But how far off are the more ambitious versions? Who knows.
<ul>
<li>In particular, because the work of designing genes (chemical pathways, regulatory networks, growth and division programs, etc.) is <em>copied</em> and not actually <em>performed</em>, you have that the final performance is perfectly <em>real</em> (the cell-with-synthetic-genome really lives; the LLM really can program computers (for some tasks)) but is <em>weirdly non-indicative of the designer's ability to design powerful artifacts</em>.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Alphafold is some sort of big advance. But does it mean you're about to get synthetic life, just because suddenly a bunch of protein folding questions went from IDK / costly to measure, to a very cheap pretty-good guess? I doubt it. There's still a ton of design work.</li>
<li>In general, you can get various sigmoids at different times, of different sizes, and of different frequency (thence, different smoothness after being all added up together).</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>There are ways to "cheat".</strong>
<ul>
<li>For AGI, there's brain uploading, or neuromorphic AI / partial uploading.</li>
<li>For synthetic life, there's e.g. JCVI's strategy of copying existing genomes.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>It's unclear how to point at specific "blockers", but there are definitely blockers</strong>, which you can tell because we aren't running around using drugs manufactured inside synthetic alien bacteria / running around being dead from AGI.</li>
<li><strong>One could easily imagine the familiar game of "goalpost moving" in this setting.</strong> E.g.:
<ul>
<li>A: What can SynLife2026 not do? How do you know it's "not really life"? What's the least impressive thing you think it won't do next year?</li>
<li>B: Sheesh, IDK. I mean, it can't process fructose right now...</li>
<li>A: [a few months later] Aha! This new paper has an oil blob with some enzymes in it that can process fructose!</li>
<li>B: Ok</li>
<li>A: So now it's synthetic life, right?</li>
<li>B: No</li>
<li>A: Something something goalposts something something complete</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>It gives some sense for why benchmarks are hard to interpret / don't necessarily say all that much.</strong>
<ul>
<li>For example, you could imagine someone creating a type of lipid that
<ul>
<li>the free forms gradually stick to each other more and more,</li>
<li>forms micelles or liposomes when aggregated,</li>
<li>and splits into multiple liposomes.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Is this progress towards synthetic life? Surely it's some kind of progress. How much? How can you tell?</li>
<li>You could make impressive videos, where the lipid-micelle-splitting video looks intuitively much more like life than what we had previously. This doesn't actually tell you very much though.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>It gives some sense for why "the bitter lesson" doesn't say all that much.</strong> Sure, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phage-assisted_continuous_evolution">phage-assisted continuous evolution</a> is very cool and outperforms human designers at least in some cases—but that doesn't really bear on whether you can just point PACE at "make synthetic life". You're still confused, just at a higher level.</li>
<li><strong>It's genuinely very unclear when it will happen.</strong> Maybe someone will announce something that seems like true synthetic life next year, but probably not; or maybe it will take 20 years or 50 years or more.</li>
<li><strong>There are plenty of ways to make substantial, legible, incremental progress on various benchmarks and subtasks.</strong>
<ul>
<li>E.g., you could invent enzymes to mimic yet another biochemical pathway or something. How much this contributes to progress on the overall task is unclear.</li>
<li>Pointing at line go up isn't that much of an argument because the issue is that you're probably pointing at the wrong lines and you haven't explained why your line is a / the right line.</li>
<li>A lot of goodharty ways of making progress don't really contribute much at all. It's not obvious, on the face of it, what the difference between goodharting and non-goodharting would be, but it's definitely a thing.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>The profile of capabilities is weird.</strong>
<ul>
<li>There's no natural bacterium with a profile of capabilities (chemical processing, resource acquisition, locomotion, defense, repair, growth) that corresponds at all well to the capabilities profile of SynLife2026 overall or of any particular instance of quasi-synthetic-life.</li>
<li>You can point to supernatural capabilities of synlife in several areas. Maybe many specific artificial chemical processing pathways are much faster / more efficient / less expensive / higher purity than biological pathways.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wgqcExv9AgN5MuJuY/bioanchors-2-electric-bacilli#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Secrets of the LessWrong RSS Feed](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dzF8vSdDtmWjCBBDr/secrets-of-the-lesswrong-rss-feed)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 23, 2026 8:12 PM GMT<br /><br /><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml">LessWrong's RSS feed</a> includes all recently published articles by default, but it has a bunch of undocumented features available with query params.</p><p>Warning: The <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dzF8vSdDtmWjCBBDr/secrets-of-the-lesswrong-rss-feed?commentId=8iGJ9thpNg6kuokcu">only officially supported options</a> are <code>view=frontpage</code>, <code>view=curated</code>, and <code>karmaThreshold</code>. Other options may break in the future.</p><ul><li>You can request only posts above a certain karma (<code>karmaThreshold</code>), only with certain authors (<code>userId</code>), frontpage only (<code>view=frontpage</code>), curated-only (<code>view=curated</code>), and several other views.<ul><li>Just the frontpage: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=frontpage">https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=frontpage</a></li><li>My posts: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?userId=piR3ZKGHEp6vqTo87">https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?userId=piR3ZKGHEp6vqTo87</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbf8hzmu50s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbf8hzmu50s">[1]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li>Comments have RSS feeds (<code>type=comment</code>, <code>view=postCommentsNew</code>, <code>postId</code>, <code>parentCommentId</code> if you want only replies to a specific comment)<ul><li>Comments on this post: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?type=comments&amp;view=postCommentsNew&amp;postId=dzF8vSdDtmWjCBBDr">https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?type=comments&amp;view=postCommentsNew&amp;postId=dzF8vSdDtmWjCBBDr</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflcvv09zhsla"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlcvv09zhsla">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li>Quick takes / shortform have an RSS feed (<code>view=shortform</code>, <code>view=topShortForm</code> or <code>view=shortformFrontpage</code>)<ul><li>Quick takes front page: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?type=comments&amp;view=shortformFrontpage">https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?type=comments&amp;view=shortformFrontpage</a></li><li>My shortform: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?type=comments&amp;view=shortform&amp;userId=6jLdWqegNefgaabhr">https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?type=comments&amp;view=shortform&amp;userId=6jLdWqegNefgaabhr</a></li></ul></li></ul><p>There's also <a href="https://gist.github.com/weidai11/7dbb2ca58b0d2dc7f36c90928cfb5150">a full GraphQL API</a> if you have needs beyond this, but the RSS feed is nice if you want it to work in a normal feed reader.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>LessWrong RSS Feed Builder</h2><p>Since there's a lot of options, I also built <a href="https://brendanlong.github.io/lesswrong-rss-builder/">a tool to generate LessWrong RSS feed URLs</a>.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dzF8vSdDtmWjCBBDr/xfih7ipscp5bioizjeef" /></figure><p>&nbsp;</p><h2>Full Documentation</h2><p>Note: This was generated by Claude after reading the <a href="https://github.com/ForumMagnum/ForumMagnum/blob/master/app/feed.xml/route.ts">source code for feed.xml</a> and <a href="https://github.com/ForumMagnum/ForumMagnum/blob/master/packages/lesswrong/server/rss.ts">related utility code</a>. Again, only <code>view=frontpage</code>, <code>view=curated</code>, and <code>karmaThreshold</code> are officially supported.</p><h2><strong>Query Parameters</strong></h2><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>Parameter</th><th>Type</th><th>Description</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>type</code></td><td>string</td><td>Set to <code>comments</code> for comment feeds, otherwise returns posts</td></tr><tr><td><code>view</code></td><td>string</td><td>Feed type/view (see views below). Defaults to <code>rss</code></td></tr><tr><td><code>karmaThreshold</code></td><td>number</td><td>Minimum karma for posts to appear (see threshold logic below)</td></tr><tr><td><code>filterSettings</code></td><td>JSON</td><td>Advanced filtering (JSON-encoded object)</td></tr><tr><td><code>postId</code></td><td>string</td><td>Filter comments to a specific post</td></tr><tr><td><code>tagId</code></td><td>string</td><td>For <code>tagRelevance</code> post view or tag comment views</td></tr><tr><td><code>userId</code></td><td>string</td><td>Filter by author user ID</td></tr><tr><td><code>parentCommentId</code></td><td>string</td><td>For <code>commentReplies</code> view - replies to a specific comment</td></tr><tr><td><code>parentAnswerId</code></td><td>string</td><td>For <code>repliesToAnswer</code> view - replies to a specific answer</td></tr><tr><td><code>topLevelCommentId</code></td><td>string</td><td>For <code>repliesToCommentThread</code> view - full thread under a comment</td></tr><tr><td><code>forumEventId</code></td><td>string</td><td>For <code>forumEventComments</code> view</td></tr><tr><td><code>sortBy</code></td><td>string</td><td>Sort mode for views that support it (see per-view notes)</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h2><strong>Post Feed Views</strong></h2><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>View</th><th>Description</th><th>Sort Order</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>rss</code> (default)</td><td>All newest posts</td><td><code>postedAt</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>frontpageRss</code></td><td>Frontpage posts only</td><td><code>frontpageDate</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>curatedRss</code></td><td>Curated posts only</td><td><code>curatedDate</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>communityRss</code></td><td>Non-frontpage posts with karma &gt; 2</td><td><code>postedAt</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>metaRss</code></td><td>Meta posts only</td><td><code>postedAt</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>tagRelevance</code></td><td>Posts by tag (requires <code>tagId</code> param)</td><td>Tag relevance score</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>Note: View names can use either camelCase (<code>frontpageRss</code>) or kebab-case (<code>frontpage-rss</code>).</p><h2><strong>Comment Feed Views</strong></h2><p>All comment views require <code>type=comments</code>. The limit is hardcoded to 50 results.</p><h3><strong>General</strong></h3><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>View</th><th>Description</th><th>Sort</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>rss</code> (default)</td><td>Recent comments with positive score (alias for <code>recentComments</code>)</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>recentComments</code></td><td>Recent comments with positive score</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>allRecentComments</code></td><td>All recent comments including neutral/negative score</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>commentReplies</code></td><td>Replies to a specific comment (requires <code>parentCommentId</code>)</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>moderatorComments</code></td><td>Comments posted with a moderator hat</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h3><strong>Per-Post (require </strong><code><strong>postId</strong></code><strong>)</strong></h3><p>These views exclude answers and answer-replies (filter <code>answer: false</code>, <code>parentAnswerId: null</code>).</p><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>View</th><th>Description</th><th>Sort</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>postCommentsNew</code></td><td>Comments on a post, newest first</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>postCommentsOld</code></td><td>Comments on a post, oldest first</td><td><code>postedAt</code> asc</td></tr><tr><td><code>postCommentsTop</code></td><td>Comments on a post, highest karma first</td><td><code>baseScore</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>postCommentsBest</code></td><td>Comments on a post, best first</td><td><code>baseScore</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>postCommentsMagic</code></td><td>Comments on a post, magic/Wilson sort</td><td><code>score</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>postCommentsRecentReplies</code></td><td>Comments on a post, by recent subthread activity</td><td><code>lastSubthreadActivity</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>postsItemComments</code></td><td>Recent non-deleted comments on a post</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>questionAnswers</code></td><td>Answers to a question post (supports <code>sortBy</code>)</td><td><code>baseScore</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>answersAndReplies</code></td><td>Answers and their replies (supports <code>sortBy</code>)</td><td><code>baseScore</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>debateResponses</code></td><td>Debate responses on a post</td><td><code>postedAt</code> asc</td></tr><tr><td><code>recentDebateResponses</code></td><td>Recent debate responses on a post</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h3><strong>Per-User</strong></h3><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>View</th><th>Description</th><th>Sort</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>profileComments</code></td><td>Comments by a user (supports <code>sortBy</code>)</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>profileRecentComments</code></td><td>(Deprecated) Recent comments by a user</td><td><code>postedAt</code> desc</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h3><strong>Shortform / Quick Takes</strong></h3><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>View</th><th>Description</th><th>Sort</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>shortform</code></td><td>Top-level shortform comments</td><td><code>lastSubthreadActivity</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>topShortform</code></td><td>Top shortform by score (supports <code>before</code>/<code>after</code>)</td><td><code>baseScore</code> desc</td></tr><tr><td><code>shortformFrontpage</code></td><td>Frontpage shortform (recent, filtered by quality)</td><td><code>score</code> desc</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h3><strong>Per-Tag (require </strong><code><strong>tagId</strong></code><strong>)</strong></h3><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>View</th><th>Description</th><th>Sort</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>tagDiscussionComments</code></td><td>Discussion comments on a tag</td><td>default</td></tr><tr><td><code>tagSubforumComments</code></td><td>Subforum comments for a tag (supports <code>sortBy</code>)</td><td><code>lastSubthreadActivity</code> desc</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h2><code><strong>sortBy</strong></code><strong> Values</strong></h2><p>Views that support the <code>sortBy</code> parameter accept these values:</p><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>Value</th><th>Sort Order</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>top</code></td><td><code>baseScore</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>new</code> / <code>newest</code></td><td><code>postedAt</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>old</code> / <code>oldest</code></td><td><code>postedAt</code> ascending</td></tr><tr><td><code>magic</code></td><td><code>score</code> descending</td></tr><tr><td><code>recentComments</code></td><td><code>lastSubthreadActivity</code> descending</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><h2><strong>Karma Threshold Logic</strong></h2><p>The <code>karmaThreshold</code> parameter determines when posts appear in the feed based on when they reached certain karma levels. Posts have timestamps for when they exceeded various karma thresholds.</p><p>Input values are rounded to the nearest supported threshold:</p><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>Input Range</th><th>Actual Threshold</th><th>Date Field Used</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>&lt; 16 (or not set)</td><td>2</td><td><code>scoreExceeded2Date</code></td></tr><tr><td>16-36</td><td>30</td><td><code>scoreExceeded30Date</code></td></tr><tr><td>37-59</td><td>45</td><td><code>scoreExceeded45Date</code></td></tr><tr><td>60-99</td><td>75</td><td><code>scoreExceeded75Date</code></td></tr><tr><td>100-161</td><td>125</td><td><code>scoreExceeded125Date</code></td></tr><tr><td>&gt;= 162</td><td>200</td><td><code>scoreExceeded200Date</code></td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>The feed item's date is the <i>later</i> of:</p><ol><li>The karma threshold date (when post reached the threshold)</li><li>The view-specific date (e.g., <code>frontpageDate</code> for frontpage feed)</li></ol><p>This allows higher-threshold feeds to show older posts that recently became popular.</p><p>&nbsp;</p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbf8hzmu50s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbf8hzmu50s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>I don't know where to get userIds from the UI, so I used the GraphQL API:</p><pre><code>$ curl -s -X POST https://www.lesswrong.com/graphql \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"query": "query { user(input: { selector: { slug: \"brendan-long\" } }) { result { _id } } }"}'
{"data":{"user":{"result":{"_id":"piR3ZKGHEp6vqTo87"}}}}</code></pre></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlcvv09zhsla"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflcvv09zhsla">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>You can find postIds in the post URL, i.e. the postId for <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioZxrP7BhS5ArK59w/did-claude-3-opus-align-itself-via-gradient-hacking">https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/<strong>ioZxrP7BhS5ArK59w</strong>/did-claude-3-opus-align-itself-via-gradient-hacking</a> is <code>ioZxrP7BhS5ArK59w</code>.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dzF8vSdDtmWjCBBDr/secrets-of-the-lesswrong-rss-feed#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI Impact Summit 2026 : A Field Report](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/ai-impact-summit-2026-a-field-report)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 23, 2026 4:58 PM GMT<br /><br /><p>This post is detailing our experience attending the <a href="https://impact.indiaai.gov.in/">AI Impact Summit</a> and its associated side events in Delhi, February 2026. We are both unfamiliar with the policy and governance domain. This is just an honest reaction attending these events, maybe there are 2nd order effects we are not privy to and we welcome feedback.</p><p>Two events ran in parallel: the main AI Impact Summit and a separate, more curated side event called <a href="https://luma.com/nsi25ret">AI Safety Connect (AISC)</a>, co-hosted with the International Association for Safe and Ethical AI (IASEAI). We attended primarily governance and policy tracks at the main summit. On the second day, we also organized an independent <a href="https://luma.com/o5f17ino">AI safety mixer</a>. This post covers what we observed across all three events, what worked, what didn't, and where we hope things would go in the future.</p><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/c58ed7fc019a36f6ae30b8c7214d2e7f409c95b466f624e8a56b05ab2adb35f1/yupofrdze5cr8oyvtizn" /></p><h2>The Main Summit</h2><h3>What Worked</h3><p>The summit's admission policy was open and inclusive. Late applications were accepted. Entry was free. Nobody was turned away. For an event of this scale that relates to AI, it really matters that this should not be a conversation restricted to a small group, and India has an opportunity to lead on broad, inclusive participation.</p><p>The summit was also useful as a reading of the room. By attending governance and policy sessions, we got insight into how different stakeholders, government officials, nonprofits, educators are orienting towards AI. We could hear what is salient to them: concerns about automation and job displacement, bias in AI systems, ownership of compute. Getting this kind of signal on world models and priorities across sectors was valuable.</p><p>I enjoyed the “Whose Language, Whose Model? Public-Interest Multilingual LLMs” by Aliya Bhatia, Center for Democracy &amp; Technology Dhanaraj Thakur, Multiracial Democracy Project because it was a workshop format, this involved breaking up the room into rows of two and we all had discussions among ourselves. This format was much better than the panel version where we just got to listen and there was limited time allotted for Q/A.</p><p>The prompt we picked was “What incentives could drive private sector AI development to be inclusive and participatory” and we talked about</p><p>1) Civil society outcry - accessibility to ensure all the stakeholders affects are able to hold companies responsible for quality</p><p>2) Regulation - how government need to ensure compliance, it helps to have benchmarks, design specs that can set expectations from industry</p><p>3) Research on how serving rare languages, and niche cultures can be a great test of out of distribution generalization. So if you want “AGI” you can be motivated to be inclusive for instrumental reasons.</p><p>4) We can trace where they are raising money from. Is it ESG funds, donations from non profits, there is value in ensuring transparency of such investments.</p><p>5) The org culture itself would be a big factor, so decisions around hiring, representation from all stakeholders affected by the technology.</p><p>I am taking a concrete thread to showcase the kind of discussions I had at the event.</p><p>The event “Cognitive Infrastructure for Sustainable and Resilient Futures” had Bertrand Badré, Dr. Saurabh Mishra, <a href="http://Taiyo.AI">Taiyo.AI</a> G. Sayeed and there were interesting parallels drawn between how reliable infrastructure projects have to be and just like the internet revolution, people will need reliable infra of AI so that others can build on top of it. So safety concepts are being borrowed from such construction-esque domains.</p><p>The “Safe AI Solutions in Education - A Practitioner-oriented Dialogue for the Global South Perspective” by Anil Ananthaswamy, IIT Madras Krishnan Narayanan, itihaasa Research &amp; Digital Shaveta Sharma had a lot of people attending it. The event had audience interactions through a slido kind of app. It was energetic to see folks participate at scale.</p><p>The “Small AI for Big Impact” had Alpan Raval, Wadhwani AI talking about deploying more local distilled smaller model at the edge that reduces dependencies on larger centralized providers, this is especially impactful in privacy sensitive domains like healthcare.</p><h3>What Was Missing</h3><p>The summit's own website was surprisingly poor for an AI-focused tech event. With hundreds of sessions, there was no effective way to filter, search, or plan attendance. There was no audience segmentation, no parallel tracks for newcomers versus experienced professionals versus academics versus school students versus entrepreneurs. Attendees were thrown into a large, complex venue with no clear signposting for context level. Day one was extremely crowded, with bottlenecked entry points: multiple queues feeding into only one or two scanners. The result was confusion and wasted time.</p><p>There were no dedicated social or networking spaces, and no structured tools like Swapcard (Maybe EAG has spoiled us) for attendee discovery. Sessions were packed back to back with no breathing room. In practice, the most productive conversations happened in queues, at random desks, or while sitting down to eat not because those were good contexts for networking, but because there was nowhere else. The probability of meeting relevant people by chance was low, and the sessions themselves (jam packed rooms) left little room for interaction.</p><p>On the topic of substance, the sessions we attended stayed at a high level. Panelists often used language that was vague and didn't convey much. Questions during Q&amp;A segments were frequently not addressed directly. There was no discussion of gradual disempowerment, economic concentration of power, or structural risk from AI; the conversation stayed within a narrow band of surface-level concern. In private, some panelists were far more candid about uncertainty and genuine risk, but on stage, the tone was managed maybe for optics. More technical AI safety people on panels would have sharpened the discourse considerably. Sessions on concrete contribution pathways, both technical and policy were absent but would have served the audience well.</p><p>A recurring theme across summit sessions was a refusal to update ontologies. In an event on AI for teaching, for instance, panelists were still operating within a framework where the teacher is the fixed authority and AI is a supplementary tool to be rationed. Some of the questions being asked should a principal decide how much time a child spends with AI? revealed a failure to reckon with how deeply this technology disrupts existing institutional structures. Even setting aside catastrophic risk scenarios, the best-case trajectory of AI capabilities demands a fundamental rethinking of what education prepares people for.</p><p>If entire job categories are heading toward automation, the response cannot be "teach students to use AI tools." It requires working backwards from which skills will remain in demand or resist automation, which in turn requires staying current with capability evaluations and model bottlenecks. The people in high decision-making positions we observed did not appear to be doing this.</p><p>Whether this stems from a top-down narrative mandate to frame AI positively, or from genuine ignorance of the pace of progress, the effect is the same: policymakers and educators are not prepared for the disruption that is already underway, even under optimistic assumptions.</p><h2>AI Safety Connect (Side Event)</h2><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/73f8eea354de3419b9aae1148fcf4cc9e0a2c6b4a18b589bd11ad28daec4d8cb/v86xhreewyyhs0fejx8k" /></p><h3>What Worked</h3><p>The <a href="https://luma.com/nsi25ret">AI Safety Connect</a> event on February 18–19 at The Imperial was a marked improvement over the main summit in organization, curation, and substance. Approximately 250 policymakers, researchers, and industry leaders attended. The venue was opulent, the group was smaller and more intimate, and the conversations were noticeably more focused. Panelists were approachable; you could walk up to Stuart Russell or researchers from FAR AI, Oxford's Martin School, or Stanford after their sessions and have substantive one-on-one exchanges about their published work, ongoing research, or collaboration opportunities. The high-bandwidth access to influential researchers and policymakers was one of the strongest features.</p><p>Several sessions stood out. The <a href="https://luma.com/i6zx8nmo">ACM TechBrief launch</a> on whether governments should buy or build their own LLMs offered a useful framework for thinking about data localization and the trust implications of API dependence. Hearing Singapore as a case study really grounded the discussion in the concrete. <a href="https://luma.com/p7zz1sy6">SaferAI presented quantitative modeling of cybersecurity risks</a> from AI misuse, with concrete metrics aimed at policymakers, a welcome departure from the vague language that dominated the main summit.</p><p>The <a href="https://luma.com/34m2i9tu">Demonstration Fair</a> featured Humane Intelligence showcasing interfaces for running evaluations, alongside demos from Apart Research, AgentiCorp, UNESCO India, and Accenture covering GenAI evaluation, AI manipulation defense, and child safety. There were more common spaces for people to meet and talk, and while things ran behind schedule, the flexibility meant people had genuine time to ask questions rather than being rushed through.</p><p>We had really amazing 1 on 1 conversations with people where we went into threat models, got some pushback on our own models within the Indian context where there is significant inertia towards adoption. It would really help if we could work with the government in ensuring they are ready for AI cyberattacks. More work like anthropic’s <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/india-brief-economic-index">economic index</a> would help.</p><p>On the second day, the <a href="https://luma.com/5rkc4khn">Shared Responsibility: Industry and Future of AI Safety</a> session surfaced important geopolitical tensions: the contradiction between advocating multilateral cooperation with China while simultaneously pushing for export controls, the difficulty of framing AI governance without the zero-sum dynamics that plague nuclear nonproliferation discourse, and the recognition that AI's massive positive upside makes aggressive stances more <a href="https://pstaidecrypted.substack.com/p/great-power-competition-and-ai-safety#:~:text=He%20explicitly%20flags%20that%20these%20dangers%20are%20in%20tension.">diplomatically costly</a> than with weapons. Hearing how DC policymakers approach lobbying and coalition-building was informative, it helped to hear how staffers are informed about development in the AI space and how it informs policy directly.</p><p>Stuart Russell gave one of the talks, moderated by Adam Gleave from FAR AI, and they discussed implementation details of inverse reinforcement learning and specific control protocols. That level of technical engagement was a welcome change.</p><h3>What Was Missing</h3><p>Despite being safety-focused, the Connect event still had significant gaps. The conversation was oriented heavily toward policymakers rather than technical people, making it difficult to network with the right counterparts if your background was technical. The <a href="https://luma.com/9nphpchn">red lines workshop</a> — formally titled "Defining and Governing Unacceptable AI Risks," building on the <a href="https://red-lines.ai/">Global Call for AI Red Lines</a> signed by over 100 leaders and Nobel laureates, covered ground that has been said many times before.</p><p>The room was largely in agreement, which raised the question of whether this consensus is actually moving the needle when the main summit doesn't prioritize safety at all. The bar for red lines was also troublingly low: "we cannot let people die" and "we cannot allow CSAM" are floors, not ceilings, and there is an enormous space of harmful outcomes above those floors that went unaddressed. There was no clear articulation of enforcement mechanisms or how to actually implement any of it.</p><p>Concentration of power was either not discussed or discussed without any clear path forward. The discourse had a quality of performed assurance, people acting as though they understood the situation more than they did. When pressed on specifics, many policymakers and even some newer researchers could not point to concrete evidence for the claims they were making about risk, or identify specific capability evaluations or reports that informed their views. It felt like a memetic capture: "safety is important" repeated as a mantra without anyone pausing to ask what safety means operationally, what the evidence base is, what the timelines are, or what capabilities they expect. One session attempted to define what safety means, but even there, only a narrow perspective was explored. A closed-door session allowed for more intense discussion, but was limited in scope and attendance.</p><p>Both the main summit and the Connect event shared a deeper failure: neither seemed to fully grasp the speed at which capabilities are advancing. The discourse felt disconnected from the pace visible on Twitter and in San Francisco, where new model releases and capability jumps are happening continuously. People at both events were still treating AI Alignment like other fields where they can defer to experts blindly, it would be better if they could acknowledge that it is a pre-paradigmatic field without established consensus.</p><p>One note on epistemics and diplomacy: some members of our community have written about the summit in ways that may not be sensitive to how the Indian government receives criticism. In policy spaces, coalition-building, relationships, and framing matter in ways that are unfamiliar and sometimes uncomfortable for people trained in EA and rationality norms. The tension between epistemic honesty and diplomatic pragmatism was something we thought more about even as we write this reflection post, we do not have easy answers. The summit's dates coinciding with Chinese Lunar New Year, resulting in China not sending top officials is one example of the kind of strategic maneuvering happening that makes cooperation harder.</p><h2>The AI Safety Mixer</h2><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/4b3e75a031c545b2f775d5b51ab2a0507ddddac72c7aa779bafa3f4e9a9711b3/d7tcogtbtnkxlonaw6ad" /></p><p>On Feb 17, we organized an independent <a href="https://luma.com/o5f17ino">AI safety mixer</a>. Around 80 people registered, and roughly thirty to forty attended. Safety was not a formal track at the summit, so this was an informal, self-organized event, funded entirely out of pocket.</p><p>The mixer opened with introductions, which initially became chaotic. We restructured into themed tables, one on risks and threat models, one on expected capabilities, one on whether AI development should be paused, and others on topics like post-AGI economy, optimistic futures and timelines. This structure helped manage the energy in the room and led to substantive, open discussions. Participants were not judgmental or defensive; the discourse was genuine and exploratory.</p><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/1c624aeeec0d78198a22292128fb0cf4b4660fd6246c2e8910eab39b821b0c62/ygqmzg5o9wlayhnrjtc0" /></p><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/f94824c8128d5587e788aaa10f11495eaa50bb0cf418effa84116e34488fbdad/raejc0uhuanvunuufgni" /></p><p>The community turned out to be larger than we expected. But it was also clear that many silos remain. There are people working on AI safety in India often don't know about each other and have no coordination point or Schelling point to converge on. This article is partly an attempt to address that. <strong>If you are working in this space or want to get involved, reach out to either of us. (</strong><a href="https://adityaarpitha.com/"><strong>Aditya</strong></a><strong> or </strong><a href="https://bhishmaraj.org/"><strong>Bhishma</strong></a><strong>)</strong> We can connect you to others in your city, invite you to communities and groups, or support you in attending future events, retreats, etc.</p><p>One concern that surfaced was gender representation. The summit itself had reasonably balanced attendance, but the safety mixer skewed noticeably. We don't yet have clear answers on why this gap exists or how to address it, but we are looking into the bottlenecks. (Please comment if you have ideas)</p><p>On logistics, we had to place fifteen to twenty people on a waiting list because the venue couldn't accommodate everyone. With proper funding and a larger venue, future events could serve the full demand. Many thanks to Ankur, Aman, Kunal for organizing this &lt;3</p><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/051d9ce08f2402a7ced12568e474fef10c6dc4ba765f678743dfbd83404d160f/p5j1u6yttpyg3gfzliiz" /></p><h2>Recommendations</h2><p>The main summit would benefit from threat-model-based and opportunity-based track design, with clear pathways organized by audience: policy professionals, technical researchers, entrepreneurs, students, newcomers.</p><p>Dedicated networking zones with structured matchmaking would dramatically improve the quality of connections made. Panels need more technical participants and a norm of honesty about uncertainty, the gap between private candor and public messaging is a problem worth naming explicitly. Decision-makers in education and governance need to engage with current capability evaluations and think forward rather than retrofitting AI into existing institutional assumptions.</p><p>The Connect event should push beyond consensus-building toward concrete implementation discussions, enforcement mechanisms. There should be space and time for examining core assumptions without loaded terminology or performative agreement.</p><p>For the AI safety community in India specifically, there is clear demand for more frequent, well-organized spaces for discussion and taking action. The mixer demonstrated that people want to engage seriously with risk, governance, and contribution pathways. What's missing is infrastructure coordination tools, funding, visibility, and a Schelling point for coordination.</p><h1> </h1><h1>Misc</h1><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/d59ed9acdb008382d5958f4d78a2df64a7baa8cc0dfd861281959537d036a68b/grbqnyuuvxl1dp8vc162" /></p><p>It was heartening to see the AI safety community self-organize so effectively. A coordination whatsapp group of around 180 members formed (thanks to Aman), and community members built <a href="https://aisummit26.info/explore">tools</a> to help sort through the <a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1CNN97vjeQ_89hW6IRRP0_MGOsjcOmK1MAx4TUhUuzTc/edit?usp=sharing">event schedule</a> which was necessary, because the <a href="https://impact.indiaai.gov.in/">official infrastructure</a> fell short on that front.</p><p>Our friend Samuel was protesting outside the summit to pause the development towards ASI. Himanshu also advocated a pause due to being concerned about risks of power concentration and surveillance by nation states. It would be encouraging to see more civil society oversight of developments in this domain.</p><p>We have reading groups in Bangalore, coworking spaces offered by <a href="https://www.electricsheep.is/">Electric Sheep</a>, and more regular events. I recently conducted <a href="https://luma.com/aqfazuka?tk=RsCZnn">an event</a> in Hyd so we can have more frequent meetups to collectively sensemake.<br /><br />Many thanks to Saksham for hosting us in the city, and thanks to Opus 4.6 for helping us draft this piece.</p><h1>Conclusion</h1><p>The summit and its side events signal real and growing institutional interest in AI safety and governance across India and internationally. That is a net positive. But the event design at the main summit didn't match the seriousness of the subject matter, and the public discourse at panels lagged well behind what participants were willing to discuss privately. The Connect event was better but still constrained by a narrow framing of safety and an orientation toward policy over technical depth. We need more events that reckon with the actual pace of capability development.</p><figure class="image"><img /></figure><figure class="image"><img /></figure><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wGjv7qhjyhvvyyWEw/ai-impact-summit-2026-a-field-report#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Knowledge Priming](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/knowledge-priming.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><b class="author">Rahul Garg</b> has observed a frustration loop when
      working with AI coding assistants - lots of code generated, but needs lots
      of fixing. He's noticed <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/">five
      patterns</a> that help improve the interaction with the LLM, and describes
      the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/knowledge-priming.html">first of these</a>: priming the LLM with knowledge about the codebase and
      preferred coding patterns.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/knowledge-priming.html">more…</a></p>
